## AI Research

### [The idiot's reductio](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YgKhExegrhax5efrJ/the-idiot-s-reductio)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Subtitle: if you truly believed that...</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every once in a while, I encounter what I call the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idiot’s reductio</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “If you truly believed X, you’d do Y. Since you don’t do Y, you must not actually believe X.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A special case is when people say “I don’t believe in/feel/have X. But if you truly did believe in X, you’d do a crazy and absurd thing. Since you don’t do this crazy and absurd thing, I conclude you don’t believe in X, and also X isn’t real.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Almost always incredibly childish and a complete failure of theory of mind. Some examples:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you actually believed in the Christian Bible, you’d avoid wearing mixed fibers. Therefore you’re a fake Christian and you should either be an atheist or believe in my specific flavor of liberal Christianity with no controversial components.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">God is the only reason for human morality. If not for Christianity, people would murder their neighbors all the time. Therefore your atheism is fake and the Christian God is real.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you actually thought abortion was murder, you’d firebomb a Walmart, and hold funerals for miscarriages. You haven’t firebombed a Walmart. QED.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you actually thought factory farming is evil, you’d firebomb a Walmart.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you actually believed in climate change, you’d firebomb a Walmart.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you actually believed in AI doom, you’d firebomb a Walmart.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Al Gore really believed in climate change, he wouldn’t fly to climate change conferences and would, I dunno, swim or something.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you really believed in open borders, you’d leave your front door unlocked and house migrants yourself.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Erika Kirk does speeches and sometimes laughs on camera in interviews. Therefore, she’s not actually sad about her husband dying. Also the death was faked.</span></li><li value="10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“She called her alleged rapist the day after, therefore she wasn’t raped.”</span></li><li value="11"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“If you actually believed in Communism you wouldn’t be using an iPhone”</span></li><li value="12"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“If Ayn Rand was a real capitalist, she wouldn’t take welfare”</span></li><li value="13"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jacob Coxon quit Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to warn the world about AI doom, he got a cacophony of comments about how if he’s actually worried he’d stay and try to make AI safer from the inside.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When Evan, head of misalignment science at Anthropic, said he’s extremely worried and that’s why he’s staying and trying to make it better, the peanut gallery immediately pivoted to saying his beliefs must be fake because otherwise he would’ve quit.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quitting is evidence of fake beliefs, and so is staying. It seems like the masters of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idiot’s reductio</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are conveniently always impervious to evidence!</span></li></ul></li><li value="14"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When </span><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2120271165692461316"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Buck wrote an emotional post about near-term AI doom</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, he got </span><a href="https://x.com/edzitron/status/2120313511331111005"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a lot of comments</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from the peanut gallery about how he’s “crying” and working on AI safety research instead of working towards pausing AI. Implicit: therefore your beliefs are fake.</span></li><li value="15"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I </span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/i-plan-to-drop-everything-to-push"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote my recent, measured post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about near-term AI doom and pivoting to pausing AI, I </span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/2121405368333185211"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">immediately</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> got dozens of aggro comments about how I should either spend my last year to convince randos of milquetoast positions on alignment, or chill out in San Diego or something. Implicit: therefore your beliefs are fake.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m sure you’ve encountered the same.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I used to think the core issue was a failure to appreciate human differences while modeling other people. Everybody approaches grief/fear/dread/betrayal/worries about humanity’s extinction in different ways. Perhaps people weren’t previously aware of this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I used to think that, but I now think the explanation is too clean, and ironically overly charitable. After thinking about it some and observing more examples in the wild, I think the failure is even worse than that: most people making the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idiot’s reductio</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> don’t even have enough imagination to model *their own* minds. Or worse, they have that imagination but choose not to use it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, if somebody says that God is the only reason that people don’t go around murdering their neighbors, I used to buy into the belief that those specific people have broken moral compasses and don’t understand how other people can have moral commitments without a divine arbitrator. But I thought about it just a tiny bit more, and realized that if those specific people de-converted, it seems unlikely they’d go around murdering people. So not only do they lack a plausible other-people-model, they also lack a coherent self-model.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, when people </span><a href="https://x.com/qualadder/status/2121820761807806938"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">are like</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “if you really believed in AI doom, you’d commit mass violence,” I used to think the only people who’d say this are psychopaths who really want to firebomb a Walmart, have minimal concept of cause and effect, are just itching for an excuse, and also incapable of modeling other people’s saner reactions.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/YgKhExegrhax5efrJ/mue6no4vej2fpso2e1uu" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A perfectly fine, unbombed, Walmart. Photo by Zack Yeo on Unsplash</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I thought about it a little bit more, and realized that of course the same people are, in addition to having poor ability/willingness to model cause and effect and other people, also (thankfully) low-agency, nonviolent, incapable of doing real stuff, etc. They, too, will not firebomb a Walmart.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the people who make the strange </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">modus tollens</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> move of “I don’t believe in/feel/have X. But if you truly did believe in X, you’d do this crazy and ridiculous and absurd thing. Since you don’t do this crazy and ridiculous and absurd thing, I conclude that not only is X false, but you also don’t believe it” sure seem off – my tentative conclusion is that they have either severely diminished reasoning faculties or severely diminished willingness to use reason, even more so than I previously thought.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not sure what the best response is in those circumstances. Two good options that come to mind are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A short, high-minded response, aimed at onlookers. “I don’t think Y is the best solution to X. Here’s what I do instead.” Limit it to 1-3 sentences.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(verbal equivalent of) Honest laughter at the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idiot’s reductio</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for being as honestly idiotic as it actually is. “Wait, wait, you’re telling me that the best way for me to reduce abortions in America is firebombing a Walmart? What a wise and moral strategy! Why didn’t I think of that?”</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bonus points if you can laugh </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">with</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your interlocutor rather than </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> them (maintaining the polite social fiction that this is an honest slip-up on their end), though that isn’t strictly required.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But either way, you should mostly not waste your time. Don’t make the mistake of taking the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">idiot’s reductio, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">or the people who voice it, seriously. “The discourse” is not real life!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YgKhExegrhax5efrJ/the-idiot-s-reductio#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A lab employee's guide to raising concerns](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JPwYH3hEfLhEDaNFp/a-lab-employee-s-guide-to-raising-concerns)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're an employee at a frontier lab, you are one of the most powerful people in the world. Not because of your nine-figure equity or the work you do—your lab </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">probably</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> won't live or die on your research: there are other researchers at the company, and if you don't refactor that eval harness, someone else will.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rather, your power comes from being able to raise your voice and speak out. If you don't speak up, either internally or externally, I'm not sure anyone else will.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your voice matters</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People say "your voice matters" as a cliche where you're one of thousands of people becoming a data point in a senator's polling, and that group perhaps moves the needle, but that's not what I mean here. I mean, the Chinese government has a file on you. You are arguably more valuable than a nuclear weapon. Just you, by yourself, literally matter a whole bunch.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don't have to email Congress or talk to journalists. You </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and they would jump at the chance to talk with you — the labs are opaque to the public yet keep saying things like "everyone on Earth could die". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you email congresspeople, many of them will be earnestly interested in what you have to say.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jacob Coxon blew up because the world was very interested in his message. If you speak out loudly, you could be in every major newspaper and go on primetime TV the next day.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6lfq7cpc42r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6lfq7cpc42r">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If instead of speaking out externally, you do an internal push, I bet that you could, by yourself, cause the company to make drastically different decisions.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure out what to push for</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few asks you could try and push for include:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Putting forward a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dBrGqjYxmidRaLLCr/how-to-pace-the-us-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">concrete pacing plan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and lobbying to mandate that pacing plan across companies.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Greater transparency, such as robust incident disclosures or embedded auditing.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taking little things your company has done wrong recently and pushing for a fix.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At OpenAI, push for a commitment to </span><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-signal-chats-that-cost-openai-researchers-their-jobs-594c5853"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">never look at employees' personal devices</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At GDM, you could have pushed them to stop making employees </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/technology/2026/11/03/google-deepmind-staff-protest-lifetime-ban-criticizing-company/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sign an agreement to never speak ill of the company for the rest of their lives</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At Anthropic, you could push for them to disclose their </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">misalignment/rogue incidents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at least as well as OpenAI has.</span></li></ol></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Being a lab employee gives you a unique viewpoint and voice</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a lab employee, you:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Know things the public doesn’t about the rate of AI progress, what incidents have occurred, and whether your company is adhering to best safety practices.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have a platform to speak inside your company and to push your company to do things— both because the company has a culture of listening to its employees (it has to, to attract top talent), and because employees listen to each other.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are legibly credible to people outside the SF tech bubble. The public, Congress, and the defense community alike don't have an easy way to evaluate AI expertise: it's hard to tell who's a quack and who's not. Having actually worked at one of these companies is an instantly legible sign that someone knows what they're talking about. As such, there's a ton of demand to speak to company employees.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is an incredible position, yet one that very few people leverage. Most people at AI companies, for whatever reason, seem either too afraid or too unsure of themselves to speak up.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I beg of you, get advice</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's hard to write a post like this. Quitting and agitating internally are spicy topics. Moreover, a lot of what makes sense to do is going to depend on the specifics of your situation and what you're comfortable with or feel you would be good at.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is very difficult to make these choices when thinking in isolation, and it's very difficult to navigate this situation if you've never seen a situation like it before. I recommend working with someone who has supported concerned employees in the past with success. I especially recommend </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jennifer Gibson of </span></b><a href="http://psst.org/" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Psst</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but I also think </span><a href="https://lessig.org/contact/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lawrence Lessig</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://thesignalsnetwork.org/contact/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Signals Network</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are great alternatives.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of these groups are attorneys, so conversations are privileged and confidential. That means it's illegal to reprimand you for talking to them, and you can share NDAed information with them if it's in the course of getting legal advice: you're no longer thinking in a vacuum!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, all those groups work pro bono, so you don't have to pay them. You can reach out to them directly (Psst via Signal @PsstLegal.01, Lessig via </span><a href="mailto:lawlessig@proton.me"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lawlessig@proton.me</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and TSN via Signal @thesignalsnetwork.01).</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recommend reaching out on a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">personal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> device. Have a low bar for reaching out. These folks would </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">love</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to hear from people earlier rather than later. In fact, if you are reading this guide, consider just reaching out to them right now!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it makes you more comfortable, you could also try reaching out to two to three friends you trust immensely who do not work at your company and talking to them (without revealing anything confidential). Or you can DM me here on LessWrong and I will help. Getting any advice is better than no advice.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Advisors will help you:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navigate any legal risks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Navigate comms if you want to talk to media. They can help you get better media or triage media that you're getting, and they can train you on how to respond to media.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reach out to important individuals to speak to, like members of Congress. (While the folks I've recommended talking to will have warm connections and be able to get you better meetings, you can also always just cold email staffers for members of Congress yourself. They will probably respond! They are very keen to talk to concerned AI company employees, but I think many people don't feel an affordance or enough comfort doing this, and so someone can help you navigate those conversations.)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They might be able to help you speak out in legally protected ways.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At a high level, they can help you brainstorm options and look out for gotchas. Of course, they won't have all the context you have, and all these advisors will want to amplify your strategic vision, but there's also a good chance they're tracking considerations you might have missed.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider being a tad cynical when planning</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think many company employees have a lot of faith and trust in their company. It's not my place here to argue about whether that trust is correct, but I would at least urge folks, when planning, to make plans that would not totally fall apart or backfire if more cynical assumptions about how these companies work hold true.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do not communicate about your plans for quitting on work devices.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If possible, go out of your way to make it abundantly legible that you are a good, reliable, scrupulous employee who is beyond reproach. Do not give your employer excuses to discredit you. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, do not be deterred if you feel like you are not a great employee. I think many people get scared out of speaking up because they think that their company or peers will discredit them. In my experience, this rarely happens. Even if you are about to get fired, I think it can still be valuable to speak up.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want to speak out about something that's not sensitive, and you want to run it by the comms team, I strongly recommend framing it as giving them a heads up, not asking for permission. Give them a deadline by which to comment. I have never seen a comms team at a lab approve anything shaped like an employee speaking out on risk. Things just get added to the pile and never resolved.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's more to life than your current job</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I find that employees at AI companies are often shockingly worried about losing their job. If you work at an AI company, you are an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extremely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> hot commodity. Many people would love to hire you. I have seen many people in this kind of position worry they will not get a job. All of them have immediately been showered with job offers. Many companies will literally try to look for when lab employees leave and then immediately try to snag them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you've spoken up and shown bravery and courage, this may make some very profit-driven large corporations more hesitant to hire you, but it will also give you an air of credibility in a space where legible, credible integrity is hard to come by. I think it will, on net, improve your career capital.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Choose whether or not to speak up internally</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're considering quitting with a bang, you could first try to raise a bunch of concerns internally in Slack and perhaps complain loudly enough that the company fires you. You could also threaten to quit if the issue is not resolved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want to stay at the company, you can also try to continuously raise concerns. Try to get a group of people together and push for whatever ask you picked above. Expand the group of people, keep pushing, and keep talking to people.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: Speaking up internally is often appealing because maintaining a collaborative culture within the company has genuine value and it's better if companies have a culture of doing the right thing. But it does have downsides. If you speak up internally, it can make it more difficult to decide to speak up externally without being identified. (If you're the person most clearly advocating for policy change X, then if it ever comes out that people outside the company are also calling for X, you will be a suspect.) People are dispositionally biased towards the easier option, and usually have too much faith in their company to change.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Choose whether or not to whistleblow</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whistleblowing means raising concerns that your company is doing something clearly reckless, unethical, or illegal, where either the world or specific policymakers are not aware and need to be.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main way to whistleblow is by raising a concern to a specific individual. Depending on the circumstances, this could be Congress, the </span><a href="https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/ai-act-whistleblower-tool"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EU AI Office</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the California Attorney General, the Securities and Exchange Commission, or another government agency. You have a lot of legal protections when talking to these folks. Whistleblowing is a legally protected right, and </span><a href="https://law-ai.org/whistleblower-protections-in-sb-53/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SB 53</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> creates some extra legal protections for whistleblowing about AI concerns in particular.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To get these legal protections, you have to go about things the right way. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do not try to figure this out yourself</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For the love of all that is holy, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I_beg_of_you__get_advice"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">talk to a whistleblowing lawyer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can also talk to journalists. If you talk to a journalist, make it clear to them that you are talking on background or off the record. If they agree to that, they will not name you. They are very protective of their sources. If you are talking to a journalist, make sure you are sharing concerns about how your company is behaving, especially dangerously, and where there is a clear public need to know. Confidentiality is important and should not be breached lightly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Whistleblower Initiative has written a guide to whistleblowing </span><a href="https://aiwi.org/guide/know-your-rights-whistleblowing-in-ai/#which-attorney-should-i-go-to" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again: If you're even vaguely considering this option, please make sure you are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I_beg_of_you__get_advice" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">getting legal advice</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794938831/lexical_client_uploads/yswki0ygh556n7gbhozz.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Choose whether or not to speak up externally</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Be the next Jacob Coxon!</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can either complain about specific practices at your company, or you can sound the alarm about AI risk more broadly. This doesn't necessarily mean critiquing your company. If you think that your company is "the good guy," you can still say you think risk is unacceptably high. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can write a tweet thread. Your tweet thread should be easy to understand and digest. I beg of you, keep it concise and to the point, and do not </span><a href="https://x.com/janleike/status/1791498174659715494" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bury the lede</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in euphemistic language that requires reading between the lines.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can talk to journalists or go on TV.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can write an </span><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/10/openai-safety-team-resignation/688881/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">op-ed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're thinking to yourself, "I have no idea how I would place an op-ed or go on TV or talk to a journalist," I will refer you to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#I_beg_of_you__get_advice" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the section on getting advice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! Those advisors know how to do it, and it's easy peasy for them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you'll be doing media or writing an op-ed, I recommend giving the comms review folks at your company a heads-up, but not asking for their permission (because they will say no or will delay/block it).</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider the optics of your actions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you raise a public fuss, you might end up with a lot of eyes on you, and your actions will reflect on AI safety as a whole.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People might read into the place you work after you quit. If you leave OpenAI complaining about safety and then go to Anthropic, that will send a very specific message, which you should think through whether you endorse. If you start talking about AI safety and then also mix in a bunch of niche political views, people might associate those views with AI safety as a whole. If you get into a fight with Elon Musk on Twitter, maybe Elon will start hating all of AI safety...</span></p><img alt="Elon Musk tweets &quot;Seems like a setup&quot;; Jacob Coxon replies &quot;I'm real and these are my real beliefs. You could ask your xAI researchers about me if you hadn't fired them.&quot;" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794006013/lexical_client_uploads/nephwd14zxrilwlkp8ib.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, these considerations should not paralyze you or hold you back from raising a fuss: inaction is generally going to be much worse for AI safety than suboptimal action! </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tips and tricks</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Remember that if/when you leave, you will lose access to most information. Therefore, if someone makes a claim like, "One time you said ABC," it will be very hard for you to refute. If you want to make claims like, "I have never said ABC," it'll be hard for you to be confident because you will not be able to check what you wrote as an employee.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are considering whistleblowing about a particular document and want to stay anonymous, opening that document many times, having a suspicious access pattern, or screenshotting that document on a work device will probably all be monitored by IT and may identify you.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But whatever you do, do something</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You have the power here. You could </span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/ai-company-employees-should-unionize"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unionize</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, you could </span><a href="https://psst.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whistleblow</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, you could agitate for a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dBrGqjYxmidRaLLCr/how-to-pace-the-us-frontier"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">concrete pacing plan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. You could write some strongly-worded complaints on Slack. But, do something.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You have the power here, but the window is closing. Every model you ship needs you a little less than the last one. It's unclear whether, in a post-AGI world, the labs will continue to exist. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do good work while you’re in there.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6lfq7cpc42r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6lfq7cpc42r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Though I think you should expect a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">somewhat</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> smaller splash than Jacob made, just because he did get a bit lucky.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JPwYH3hEfLhEDaNFp/a-lab-employee-s-guide-to-raising-concerns#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Jensen Huang Really Liked Selling China Advanced AI Chips](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/jensen-huang-really-liked-selling-china-advanced-ai-chips)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This scandal is a really big deal. <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/jensen-huang-resigns-after-having"><strong>Jensen Huang has resigned</strong></a> after multiple affairs came to light, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/nvidia-ceo-jensen-huang-had-an-affair"><strong>one of them with alleged Chinese spy Alice Huang</strong></a> who also helped smuggle billions in Nvidia chips, and another with a direct report at Nvidia.</p>
<p>The most important scandal around Jensen Huang <a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2121419369364496616">has been known for a long time to those paying attention</a>.</p>
<p>Jensen Huang and Nvidia have for years treated America and the United States Government as adversaries. They have engaged in a long running and brutal lobbying and influence campaign, both privately and publicly, to capture relevant government policy to allow Nvidia to sell as many as possible of its most advanced chips to China, and to allow China to otherwise use those chips via a combination of chip smuggling and remote access.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25800"></span>


</div>
<p>They have compromised the integrity of our government, potentially quite broadly, as well as the national security of the United States of America. They have greatly hurt our relative position in the ‘AI race.’ In addition to the considerations of national security and our strategic interest, this has forced our AI companies to push forward faster and harder, and made sane AI policy much harder.</p>
<p>When Jensen Huang chose to have a long-running affair with Alice Huang, he knew that she was central to the diversion of billions of dollars in high end AI chips to China. His company was active in covering up those actions and in clamping down on official investigations and enforcement. He did not need to suspect or know she was a spy to know the nature of his actions regarding her, or his other actions and rhetoric and those of Nvidia in general, and how they directly hurt the national security of the United States.</p>
<p>The nice term we used to use for this, back when anyone seemed to care, was corruption. There is also the less nice term that we used to use for such things.</p>
<p>Even on Monday we learned of yet another covert influence operation by Nvidia to promote them selling chips to China in order to ‘<a href="https://x.com/TheMidasProj/status/2122347325775638549">ensure US, not Chinese-made chips, continue to dominate the AI market.</a>’</p>
<p>After several days in which Alice Huang’s status as a Chinese spy was not in dispute, we now have pushback from various sources that Alice Huang may not have formally been a Chinese spy. The ODNI people near Clayton called that description ‘not quite accurate.’ The CIA sources say the intercepts do not ‘show her knowingly pass information to China.’ Himes expressed doubt.</p>
<p>It is possible that she was not formally recruited, and did not share intel with the CCP. If true, this hopefully would mean there was not additional loss of intelligence or other things due to this relationship. I would put my current p(spy) at clearly over 50%, but less than 95%, and do not see much value in trying to refine further.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2122337394917826973">Trump is now saying he doesn’t know she’s a Chinese spy</a>, after many days of (like everyone else) conspicuously not saying that. In my experience, if we did not have good reason to presume she was a spy, it would not have taken this long for people to start denying it. Other than Himes, those denying the reports have a direct interest in casting doubt on her status as a spy, including to explain their own inaction. Himes said ‘lawmakers still lack enough to tell’ how Alice acted, but that is entirely consistent with simply not having been sufficiently briefed.</p>
<p>So okay, <a href="https://www.patreon.com/MaxRead/posts/mapping-jensen-175257766">now the arrow says ‘suspected of’ spying, out of an abundance of caution</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/blgr8lbzuhmo1hjdgt6l" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Does putting ‘suspected of’ there change the overall picture? Is that the only thing you should put in that arrow, as opposed to for example ‘smuggled chips for’?</p>
<p>(Also, this is missing the third ‘alleged’ affair. There’s always more stuff.)</p>
<p>Or this graph, <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-13/banned-nvidia-chips-installed-at-china-data-center-linked-to-alice-huang">originally from Bloomberg</a>?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/gvdoahnacnp7kd771iap" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>Even if it turns out she was not part of the formal Chinese spy network, she was acting as a de facto Chinese agent. Her primary mode of doing business was still the smuggling of billions of dollars of Nvidia’s top AI chips, and allowing China to access them. It also does not change what Jensen Huang should have believed or done from his epistemic position.</p>
<p>Nor does it change that he had three affairs that we know about, one of which was with a major customer illegally smuggling billions in chips and the other two with Nvidia employees including at least one direct report.</p>
<p>If you wish, you can retroactively qualify or modify all my related statements about Alice Huang accordingly. Given her role in chip smuggling and sales, I do not believe this materially changes my conclusions about Nvidia or Jensen Huang.</p>
