<h4>The Story That Matters Most</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2121419369364496616">Miles Brundage is correct to draw the parallel to the situation with Jacob Coxon</a>. Where everyone paying attention has known the basics for a while but we needed a precipitating event to make Nvidia’s and Jensen Huang’s bad faith actions common knowledge.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DAlperovitch/status/2121207067000058127/history">Dmitri Alperovitch</a>: Whether or not Alice Huang had been recruited by the Chinese Ministry of State Security has always been a tertiary issue.</p>



<p>What’s quite clear is that:&nbsp;</p>
<ol>
<li>She has been a large Nvidia customer (and a sexual relationship with her is highly problematic from a corporate policy and governance perspective for Jensen on those grounds alone)</li>
<li>She is controlling a shell company Megaspeed dedicated to smuggling compute to both US Govenment-listed Chinese AI companies and to data center build outs in China itself – all in serious violation of US law, potentially making Jensen part of a criminal conspiracy to evade US export controls.</li>
<li>She is connected to an entire web of Chinese shell companies trying to do the same.</li>
</ol>
<p>Jensen wasn’t fired because he had an affair with an alleged MSS agent. He was fired because he:</p>
<ol>
<li>Had at least one and now, quite plausibly, multiple affairs with people in his reporting chain in violation of company policy. Something that has gotten numerous CEOs fired over the years, including at Intel and McDonalds.</li>
<li>Lied to his board about his relationship and forced the company to put out false statements to the public, putting it in legal jeopardy.</li>
<li>Was involved in a sexual relationship with a Chinese smuggler that had violated US export controls laws and provided her with billions of dollars of Nvidia chips that facilitate that criminal activity.</li>
<li>By lying and hiding that relationship from his family, the company, and the public, he put himself in a position to be blackmailed by Chinese intelligence services.</li>
<li>He very likely ordered his general counsel to interfere in an investigation by US government into his mistress’ smuggling operation.</li>
<li>He presided over stunning and extensive compliance failures to investigate and prevent his chips from being smuggled illegally into Chinese mainland and being provided to PLA affiliated institutions, all in violation of US law</li>
</ol>



<p>This is a shocking indictment of him and his record and the board clearly had no alternative but to fire him.&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2121244577008848953">Jessica Tillipman</a>: And this is why Nvidia will be one of my leading case studies in my anti-corruption and compliance class this spring.</p>



<p>Few case studies include so many issues at the same time: conflicts of interest, third party due diligence, corporate compliance policies, tone at the top, corporate governance, board fiduciary duties, risk management, corporate disclosure obligations, internal investigations, and a growing number of government ethics and accountability issues that we learn more about every day.&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Jefbak/status/2121304713799020650">Jeff Baker Films</a>: What is your take on Jensen resigning because otherwise he would have to sign SOX filings for the earnings report next week and that would have left him criminally liable?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2121308472474042862">Jessica Tillipman</a>: Given what has been reported/alleged, he may already have substantial personal exposure that doesn’t depend on the next filing. So no, I don’t think avoiding the next SOX certification is the primary driver of his resignation.</p></blockquote>
<p>The reason I keep Mike Solana on as a source, despite all his rhetoric, is that underneath it all he calls it like he sees it, so we get statements like this one:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/micsolana/status/2121267911079924038">Mike Solana</a>: funny that people are blaming jensen’s fall on both anti-asian bigotry, in the reflexively woke corner of the internet, and on something more akin to ‘me too’ overreaching on the based right. anyway, fwiw I think it mostly has to do with the fact that he was fucking a ccp asset.</p>
<p>probably it wouldn’t matter if the CEO of olive garden accidentally fucked a spy. mistakes happen. boys will be boys! but we are in an AGI arms race with china, are we not? and they are trying to thwart export controls, are they not? and jensen is a key figure here, is he not?</p>
<p>ftr if jensen really did just have a few consensual affairs, the megaspeed / ccp stuff is fabricated, or jensen really didn’t suspect anything, and we discover a reasonable motive for the CIA to destroy him, i’m on board with the persecution narrative. just don’t see that yet.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Yes Export Controls Matter and Are Finally Happening</h4>


<p>Something about a goose chasing you asking why the stocks went down.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Noahpinion/status/2121357569306644802">Noah Smith</a>: Export controls work, and all the people who say “export controls will just make a Chinese tech stronger” are either fools or liars</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/FT/status/2120837487761207521">Financial Times</a>: China warns US not to use Nvidia scandal as pretext for tech curbs</p>
<p>Shares in Chinese tech giants that have relied on Nvidia chips for developing their AI models fell on Thursday, with Alibaba down 6.8 per cent.</p>
<p>Two Chinese large language model providers listed in Hong Kong, Z.ai and MiniMax, sank more than 9 per cent and nearly 8 per cent respectively.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/kyleichan/status/2121050139729240533">Kyle Chan</a>: The market believes Chinese AI companies are still highly dependent on Nvidia chips.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2122694748666528168">Joe Weisenthal</a>: I hadn’t thought to look at the publicly traded Chinese AI companies. But the maker of GLM 5.3 is down by nearly 33% in the last 4 trading days.</p>
<p>Oof, the stock is down nearly 89% from its peak this summer.</p></blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang no longer being CEO of Nvidia is crashing the value of Z.ai. Why?</p>
<p>You know exactly why.</p>
<p>We kept not getting new export controls because Howard Lutnick kept not allowing them to go forward, <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2122496525339414796">forcing Scott Bessent and the NSC to finally go around him</a> in <a href="https://www.wsj.com/politics/national-security/commerce-department-moves-to-crack-down-on-chip-exports-65b8f3c2">order to issue a remote access rule</a> and to expand restrictions on Malaysia, Thailand, Indonesia, Singapore and Brazil.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/politics/national-security/commerce-department-moves-to-crack-down-on-chip-exports-65b8f3c2">Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): “The rule is designed to ensure that existing restrictions on access to sensitive American technology cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in third countries,” the Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security said in a release.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RushDoshi/status/2122320911290278377">Rush Doshi</a>: The Trump administration <a href="https://bis.gov/press-release/bis-issues-rule-addressing-remote-access-american-super-intelligence-technology">just dropped new remote access rules</a>, no doubt spurred by the Nvidia scandal. In the first paragraph, they made Commerce publish a line effectively indicating the Administration’s lack of faith in Commerce. Remarkable.</p></blockquote>
<p>This way of getting the rule finalized was an unprecedented move, forced by unprecedented refusal to follow either the national interest or the wishes of the President.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/politics/national-security/commerce-department-moves-to-crack-down-on-chip-exports-65b8f3c2">Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): “It is unprecedented for a Commerce Department rule to be written by the National Security Council and the Treasury Department,” said Chris McGuire, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations who previously worked on tech and security issues at the State Department. “The fact that the Commerce Department press release says this shows that Commerce resisted publishing this rule to the bitter end, and was forced to do so by the White House.”</p></blockquote>
<p>We badly need these rules. Nvidia did not even cut off specifically Megaspeed, after they were caught and supposedly put on a ‘no sell’ list, to the extent that this was not even hidden from trade records:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AnaSwanson/status/2122562163898237096">Ana Swanson</a>: Both Nvidia and the Commerce Department have now said that Nvidia placed Megaspeed on a “no sell” list in June 2025.<br />
<a href="https://t.co/1vx8Nl5Gf0">But trade data the Times obtained from @importgenius show that</a>, between July 2025 and February 2026, Megaspeed’s Malaysian subsidiary received more than $800 million of shipments containing controlled Nvidia chips.</p></blockquote>
<p>Nvidia’s board may at least be pretending to do something about this, now. They have announced a new Oversights Committee. That’ll <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-board-of-directors-establishes-national-security-and-export-control-oversight-committee">Nvidia Newsroom</a>: NVIDIA Board of Directors Establishes National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee</p>
<p><strong>News Summary</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>Committee to provide oversight over NVIDIA’s national security and global trade compliance programs</li>
<li>Committee will retain independent, outside experts to provide legal, national security and trade compliance expertise to support the Committee</li>
<li>Director Suzanne Nora Johnson appointed Committee Chair</li>
</ul>
<p>NVIDIA (NASDAQ: NVDA) today announced that the Board of Directors has established the National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee, a new standing committee responsible for board-level oversight of the company’s national security policies, export control compliance and global trade risk management. NVIDIA’s existing National Security Team and Global Trade Compliance Team will report directly to the Committee and to Interim CEO, Colette Kress, on day-to-day operations.</p>
<p>The Committee will be chaired by director Suzanne Nora Johnson and will also include directors Stephen C. Neal and John O. Dabiri as members. It will meet at least quarterly, including with management and in executive session. The Committee will oversee the Company’s ongoing efforts to enhance its internal National Security Team and Global Trade Compliance Team, and work closely with independent third-party experts in law, national security and global trade compliance.</p>
<p>“NVIDIA recognizes that advanced silicon technologies and the emergence of artificial and superintelligence present unprecedented challenges for national security. As the world’s leading advanced computing company, NVIDIA has both a responsibility and an opportunity to lead the industry in addressing these challenges. We are committed to working constructively with government and industry partners to help establish and advance a robust framework that uses every tool at our disposal to safeguard national security while supporting continued technological innovation,” said Harvey Jones, Chairman of NVIDIA’s Board of Directors.</p>
<p>The decision to create this new committee reflects the Board’s further commitment to enhance its engagement with relevant government stakeholders, and to demonstrate the highest standards of governance and oversight.</p></blockquote>


<h4>How Far Does This Go?</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/the-nvidia-story-is-a-bfd">As Nate Silver says, this story is a really big deal</a>. He argues that the political world, partly due to this being a busy news cycle, is still underreacting to this story. This includes the risk of a major anti-AI backlash.</p>
<p>Potentially this goes all the way to the top. Congress will be investigating.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ollywiseman/status/2121307701695168955">There is a strong case</a> that this is the most serious scandal of the Trump era, full stop.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ollywiseman/status/2121307701695168955">Oliver Wiseman</a>: “We spoke with three former Commerce officials who described being sidelined or pushed out of the department during Trump’s term after devising technology restrictions aimed at protecting U.S. national security. One said there was a perception in the department that Nvidia had a hand in at least some of the purges.”</p>



<p><a href="https://www.thefp.com/p/is-this-the-most-serious-scandal-of-trump-era-jensen-huang-nvidia?hide_intro_popup=true">Must read on the Nvidia scandal from Matt Pottinger and Liza Tobin</a>.&nbsp;</p></blockquote>
<p>It almost certainly goes as far as Howard Lutnick. Hopefully Lutnick gets removed.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/12/us/politics/lutnick-nvidia-commerce-china-chips.html">Ana Swanson, Tripp Mickle, Maggie Haberman and Jonathan Swan</a> (NYTimes):</p>
<p>There is broad agreement among most senior Trump administration officials that the United States should more aggressively tighten controls on Nvidia’s advanced chips to keep China from using them to catch up in the race to dominate artificial intelligence.</p>
<p>Then there is Howard Lutnick, who runs the Commerce Department.</p>
<p>… Within the Commerce Department, Nvidia is treated differently than other firms, current and former officials say, with its issues given high priority and sent through specific channels.</p>
<p>Mr. Lutnick frequently calls and messages with Mr. Huang, and the department prepares carefully for their private meetings together in Mr. Lutnick’s fifth floor office. All Nvidia matters are routed through Jeff Kessler, a top deputy to Mr. Lutnick, who is said to rarely take any significant action without the commerce secretary’s approval.</p>
<p>Nvidia’s general counsel has also been given direct access to shape new rules, including leaving comments in draft regulations affecting the company.</p>
<p>… Mr. Lutnick has repeatedly delayed or deferred technology controls, slowing down efforts throughout the year, according to people who described the discussions.</p></blockquote>
<p>Straight up corruption is a plausible hypothesis.</p>
<blockquote><p>… There are also concerns about possible conflicts of interest given that the investment firm that Mr. Lutnick steered for decades, Cantor Fitzgerald, <a href="https://archive.is/o/yNYIm/https://www.nytimes.com/2025/11/20/us/politics/howard-lutnick-family-ai.html">operates in a wide range of industries</a>, from cryptocurrency to data centers, that rely on Nvidia chips and could be affected by rules Mr. Lutnick is currently weighing. That business is now run by Mr. Lutnick’s sons.</p>
<p>… The activities of Mr. Lutnick’s sons have been a source of irritation for Mr. Trump and concern for some other Trump advisers, despite the fact that Mr. Trump’s own sons’ businesses have prompted similar criticisms.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlecStapp/status/2121401144039596060">Alec Stapp</a>: <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/12/us/politics/lutnick-nvidia-commerce-china-chips.html">Key points from @ChrisRMcGuire</a>:</p>



<p>1. “It was Mr. Lutnick who made the decision not to enforce export controls designed to counter chip smuggling, making Mr. Huang a potential target.”</p>



<p>2. “It is largely Mr. Lutnick’s fault that Megaspeed, the company that employed the alleged spy, successfully smuggled a reported $7 billion of Nvidia chips into China (a number that no doubt represents the tip of the iceberg).”</p>



<p>3. “Chip smuggling is running rampant. Taiwanese customs data shows that from 2025 to 2026, over $60 billion worth of A.I. chips were most likely shipped to Singapore, Malaysia and Thailand, making these 3 relatively small countries among the largest importers of A.I. chips in the world.”</p>



<p>4. “Mr. Lutnick has not added a single company to the entity list, the Commerce Department’s trade blacklist, in over a year, which is the longest stretch without an update in 18 years.”</p>



<p>5. “Mr. Lutnick was reportedly briefed on Mr. Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang (no relation), a Megaspeed executive, and her suspected ties with Chinese intelligence services, in May 2026.”</p>



<p>6. “Mr. Lutnick appears to be holding fast on keeping Megaspeed off the blacklist, despite repeated urgings from other members of the administration — including Mr. Trump himself.”</p>



<p>7. “Even worse, senior officials under Mr. Lutnick may have impeded law enforcement investigations into Megaspeed. In May 2025, Jeff Kessler, a top deputy to Mr. Lutnick who oversees export controls, reportedly discussed the department’s ongoing investigation into Megaspeed with Nvidia’s general counsel — a highly unusual action. The department halted its investigation soon afterward.”&nbsp;</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://daringfireball.net/linked/2026/11/13/sacks">Potentially it could reach David Sacks</a>, who was the AI Czar and in that position was very clearly de facto functioning as Nvidia’s chief lobbyist and advocate.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KareemRifai/status/2121682888613822524">Kareem Rifai</a>: I think there should be a lot more questions being asked about David Sacks, who spent years furiously lobbying for the US to induce Ukraine’s surrender and then used his position in government to lobby on behalf of giving the CCP unfettered access to Nvidia chips.</p></blockquote>


<h4>The First Step Is Admitting You Have a Problem</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-13/inside-jensen-huang-s-final-hours-as-the-ceo-of-nvidia">I find this part of the story very much to Huang’s credit</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/wallstengine/status/2121057521544958152">Wall St Engine</a>: Jensen Huang in his last hours as Nvidia CEO, per Bloomberg:</p>



<p>&gt; The board met late into the night &amp; left him no choice but to resign<br />
&gt;<br />
&gt; He looked broken, his take-charge style gone<br />
&gt; Started saying things like “whatever” &amp; “I don’t care”<br />
&gt; He has since called himself a “piece of sh–”<br />
&gt; He says he no longer cares what happens to him<br />
&gt; His main concern: his wife, and not “ruining the lives” of the women now linked to him&nbsp;</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/oaixw6zu9wmvzfhj7wmi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p>​<a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-13/inside-jensen-huang-s-final-hours-as-the-ceo-of-nvidia">Drake Bennett and Ian King</a> (Bloomberg): A turning point came last Friday, Nov. 6, when a Financial Times story cited US officials saying that the relationship had been a romantic one. Soon afterward, Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter went to Huang’s house to confront him, and Huang confessed. At that point, most of the board still didn’t want to take a public stance, but Teter, who is secretary of the board, told them they had to act.</p>
<p>… On Wednesday, the New York Times reported that Huang was also having an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care. This time, the board acted quickly. Directors assembled remotely on Wednesday, and the meeting lasted late into the night. In the end, Huang was given no choice but to resign, leaving him with no formal role at the company he co-founded.</p></blockquote>
<p>Alas, he loses most of that credit the minute he starts planning a potential comeback.</p>


<h4>Somehow People Are Still Defending Jensen Huang</h4>


<p>There is a logic that goes something like this, and it generalizes beyond Huang:</p>
<ol>
<li>Jensen Huang did these no good, quite bad things, sure.</li>
<li>But they never would have come to light like this, and he would have gotten away with it, except for those meddling kids, who told the truth about it.</li>
<li>Thus this is character assassination, a government plot, tyranny, must not stand.</li>
</ol>
<p>This comes from a cultural position that of course anyone powerful will have done many horrible things, and of course we do not hold them accountable, so trying to hold them accountable by saying the things they have done must be some evil plot.</p>
<p>I disagree.</p>
<p>There are also arguments that take this form:</p>
<ol>
<li>Jensen Huang was the founder and CEO.</li>
<li>Therefore, Nvidia is his, and if you take it away from him, you need proof that he did something sufficiently horrible, and it’s like a court of law. If it turns out you overcharged him then double jeopardy attaches and he goes free on the lesser included offenses. Which in this case are actually far worse but never mind that.</li>
<li>But you can’t formally prove the exact thing in question, so he walks, which means you have to let him continue to run Nvidia. This is an outrage.</li>
<li>I mean, come on, OpenAI’s board couldn’t fire Sam Altman, and also I would really like if my board couldn’t fire me. So let’s bring Jensen Huang back.</li>
</ol>
<p>No, seriously. As in:</p>
<blockquote><p>Garry Tan: Jensen deserves due process, not whatever is happening right now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/catehall/status/2121703342598721665">Cate Hall</a>: “what about DUE PROCESS??” is a really funny response to the duly authorized board of a private company making the core decision it exists to make.</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/tcdqm6rlimd5eq8pfpec" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Again, I disagree. This is madness.</p>
<p>The central purpose of the board of directors is to, if necessary, fire the CEO.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/PalmerLuckey/status/2120770412741263858/quotes">You also have this great illustration of a deeply twisted worldview twice over:</a></p>
<blockquote><p>Palmer Luckey (CEO Anduril): China wants Jensen out, so I want him in.​</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/h7ckk9ffw9pide14p7nl" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>China does not want Jensen Huang out, or at least I would be extremely surprised and confused to learn that China wanted that. Jensen Huang has at minimum been quite the ‘useful idiot’ selling them billions in chips, with this scandal and his departure triggering renewed strong calls for export controls on chips. See the section ‘Yes Export Controls Matter’ which is one of a highly continuing series. Why would China want him out?</p>
<p>Then there’s the second level. Why do you think it is a good argument, or virtuous, or that it speaks well of you, to publicly say that you want Jensen in because China wants him out? That you think this is a strong argument, without looking at the reasons? That you should want whatever the other guy does not want, because the other guy is a bad man?</p>
<p>Which in turn makes you make up stuff about what the other guy wants.</p>
<p>I like to think Palmer Luckey does not actually think like that, and instead only thinks that it is beneficial to his social position to present himself as thinking like this, and thus is lying. If he genuinely does think that way, this is far worse.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2121601781188936074">Teortaxes tries to maintain that what ‘has been done</a>’ to Jensen is ‘significantly worse than what happened to Jack Ma.’ Nothing ‘was done’ to Jensen other than to reveal things he most definitely did, and to fire him for those actions. Jensen Huang remains a free man with many billions of dollars. At most, there were overconfident statements about the status of Alice Huang. The rest is clearly true, and the Nvidia board are the ones who removed Jensen Huang. His position was clearly untenable, and would have been untenable either way.</p>
<p>I thought all of that was as crazy as it would get. Boy was I wrong. I happened to see this post by Ben Thompson, ‘Obituaries,’ in which he calls this the death of, and I swear I am not making this up:</p>
<ol>
<li>Jensen Huang as CEO of Nvidia, okay, I guess.</li>
<li>Nvidia, most valuable company in the world, not technically accurate, but ok.</li>
<li>The AI bubble, ‘2025-2027’, ?!, sigh.</li>
<li>AI as normal technology and the US Semiconductor industry, 1947-2047, ???.</li>
<li>Tech company independence, 1968-2026, no, seriously.</li>
<li>American exceptionalism, 1776-2026, no, seriously.</li>
</ol>
<p>What does one even say to something like that, when what happened was ‘tech CEO who treated America as an adversary and facilitated the smuggling of billions in chips while sleeping with the executive in question who is probably also a spy and also a direct report gets summarily and appropriately forced to resign and replaced by the CFO while a search begins, and the Treasury Secretary asks for a new CEO from outside in light of all the rampant crime facilitation and national security issues.’</p>
<p>If this is the end of American exceptionalism or tech company independence, what about [unfurls comically long scroll of events from the second Trump administration, the Biden administration, the first Trump administration, the Obama administration…]</p>
<p><a href="https://www.patreon.com/MaxRead/posts/mapping-jensen-175257766">Max Read is on point on why some people cannot let this one g</a>o, no matter how obviously Jensen Huang is now unfit to be CEO of Nvidia, and how badly this compromised national security.</p>
<blockquote><p>Max Reed: Two ongoing debates in particular, one central to the White House and the other to the AI sector, have been already shaping this story for over a year now:</p>
<ol>
<li>China hawks vs. (expedient) China doves.</li>
<li>Open weight vs. closed weight models.</li>
<li><a href="https://x.com/readmaxread/status/2121960731910565924">Founder class vs. managerial class.</a></li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>To those in the vibe army of the Silicon Valley founder class, they are visionaries who will save the world. Any limits whatsoever on their power, any attempt to impose any form of control on them, is tyranny. Unless you are talking about Dario Amodei, who is himself considered tyranny. All other founder CEOs are to be as Gods.</p>
<p>The China dove part is obvious. The weird part about it is that the main reason to be a China dove is so that Nvidia can sell more chips and thus make more money. You know, for the ‘American tech stack.’ That whole China dove position is an Nvidia op first, and only secondarily a China op. Two astronauts.</p>
<p>This is then piled on top of the open weight support. Jensen Huang supports open weights, not out of ideology but because he wants to commoditize his complement and does not understand the nature of the risks involved. So these people love him, thinking this is more than an expedient thing, or that a new Nvidia CEO might act differently on that front.</p>
<p>If I was in the AI or tech industry and thinking strategically, I would not want to make it look like the tech industry was rallying around Jensen Huang. That would substantially increase the chance and size of major political backlash.</p>


<h4>Jensen Huang Wants to Call It a Comeback</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SingularityChad/status/2122392132342153509">Singularity Memes Highlights</a>: they did the meme:</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/sqftyaho8a4jqfkywgih" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-15/huang-discusses-comeback-as-nvidia-chief-with-allies-ft-says?fromMostRead=true">He has discussed this potential comeback with allies.</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-15/bessent-says-nvidia-needs-dramatic-shakeup-after-huang-ouster">Tony Czuczka</a> (Bloomberg): While Nvidia shares fell 12.7% last week, some shareholders expressed support for Jensen Huang, including his possible return to the company he helped turn into one of the world’s most valuable companies.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GavinSBaker/status/2121401397346521412">One of those allies is Gavin Baker, calling for Jensen Huang’s reinstatement</a>, including supporting his reinstatement now pending the investigation. <a href="https://x.com/balajis/status/2122093003116519827">Balaji of course is on the train as well</a>, trying to vibe and threaten Jensen back into power in various ways and grasping at straws. As are Elon Musk, Travis Kalanick and over 170,000 signatories.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/business/nvidia-board-warned-of-irreplaceable-talent-loss-in-employee-letter-b9783b1e">They also arranged for a letter warning that ‘irreplaceable’ talent could be lost</a> if Jensen Huang is not reinstated. There are always such warnings, and indeed some employees do leave when the CEO changes. That’s life.</p>
<p>These people are even betting that this could happen. <a href="https://polymarket.com/event/jensen-huang-rejoins-nvidia-byptptpt">As in</a>, <a href="https://polymarket.com/event/next-ceo-of-nvidia">see Polymarket</a>, where people are buying Jensen Huang remaining as CEO at 16% (!?):</p>
<div>
<div>
<div></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>
<div>
<div></div>
</div>
</div>
<p>No, sir. This is not going to happen. Even if the board were somehow willing to cave on this, and even if Trump says otherwise and is saying Huang ‘learned a big lesson,’ the rest of Washington is absolutely not going to allow Jensen Huang to run Nvidia.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/business/nvidias-board-not-considering-reinstating-jensen-huang-as-ceo-a2b913b1?mod=WTRN_pos1">The board, knowing this and also for other very good reasons, is having none of it</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2122385953805472175">Andrew Curran</a>: CNBC is reporting that the NVIDIA board is focused on a national security investigation into Jensen Huang, and is not in discussions with him, or considering bringing him back as CEO.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/market-data/stock/nvda?mod=article_chiclet">Robbie Whelan and Amrith Ramkumar</a> (WSJ): <a href="https://www.wsj.com/market-data/stock/nvda?mod=article_chiclet">Nvidia’s</a> board of directors isn’t considering reinstating <a href="https://www.wsj.com/topics/person/jensen-huang">Jensen Huang</a> as chief executive, despite a drumbeat of calls for his return from some investors and employees, according to a source familiar with the board’s thinking.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is the kind of thing that makes DC think that the tech industry is completely clueless and also hostile and dangerous. They flat out don’t get it.</p>
<p>Or maybe I am the one who is wrong, because it is 2026 and literally nothing matters.</p>
<p>There are plenty of out of line actions to go around. Donald Trump was out of line and being deeply silly to suggest a specific person, Tim Cook, take over Nvidia. <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-11-15/bessent-says-nvidia-needs-dramatic-shakeup-after-huang-ouster">Bessent was far less out of line, but still out of line, to tell Nvidia to pick an outsider</a> and a ‘dramatic shakeup.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/annmarie/status/2121990105875312658">Annmarie Hordern</a>: Secretary Bessent to Fox: “I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?”</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2121992239991964031">As Joe Weisenthal says, can you imagine if Elizabeth Warren was the one saying that</a>?</p>
<p>But also, the man has a point. If your company facilitates this level of blatantly illegal activity on matters vital to national security, I can see why you might need an outsider.</p>
<p>OpenAI and Anthropic and SpaceX and Meta and so on have these different special governing structures, in theory or in practice, part of which is to make the CEO harder to fire. <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/opinion/newsletters/2026-11-16/people-miss-jensen-huang-already">Matt Levine has fun going over the alternative versions of this crisis</a>. I think he has too much fun. Sam Altman was able to be un-fired, but only because (among other reasons) the board was unable to explain its reasoning and the employees responded with an open revolt. In this case, the reasoning is very clear, and it is also clear to the national security state.</p>
<p>The board might try to not fire Elon Musk in a similar situation. Good luck with that.</p>
<p>If I was Jensen Huang, I would be less worried about trying to return as CEO and more worried about cooperation, and the potential civil and criminal implications, and hopefully also I would care about national security or even humans not all dying.</p>


<h4>On Interim CEO Kress</h4>


<p>I agree with Joe’s interpretation here of WSJ’s description of events.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2121278897899602248">Joe Weisenthal</a>: <a href="https://wsj.com/tech/jensen-huang-colette-kress-nvidia-ceo-eb289097?mod=hp_twoviz_pos_1">This WSJ story on Nvidia‘s interim CEO is interesting</a>. The headline frames it around personality. A clash between Huang and Kress. But actually it seems like a fundamentals story. That there’s a sense of stress within the company about its growing leverage.</p></blockquote>
<p>Jensen Huang’s style was highly abrasive. I think the WSJ article was painting this disagreement into something that it was not, and that it does not provide much evidence of Jensen Huang’s central opinions about Kress.</p>


<h4>An Actual Attack on Jensen’s Character</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pw_mcgovern/status/2121118282807918867/history">Pat McGovern</a>: from jensen’s biographer ( @stephenwitt ) in today’s NYT</p>
<p>Stephen Witt: Mr. Huang went to considerable lengths to cloak his arrogance. When I first met him in 2023, he and his handlers were pushing the idea that he was a humble man. In our conversations, Mr. Huang was constantly deflecting attention away from his success, always quick to assign credit to his employees and executive staff members.</p>
<p>He also liked to glad-hand service people. He’d been a dishwasher himself once, a point he never let you forget. He loved visiting small, hole-in-the-wall restaurants and leaving extravagant tips. I’d met him for breakfast at Denny’s, and he tipped our waitress $1,000 in hundred-dollar bills. He didn’t make a show of it, either. I had to track her down to ask.</p>
<p>Over time, though, I began to suspect that this was an act, one performed not for the observer, but to flatter himself.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Austen/status/2121282408674988257">Austen Allred</a>: If I were a billionaire I would quietly tip $1,000 all the time and I would get a lot of pleasure out of it. I just don’t understand how that makes someone arrogant.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, if you are quietly tipping waitresses $1,000 you are not doing it as an Effective Altruist. You are largely doing it because you feel great doing it. I don’t think it is an ‘act,’ even if it is largely a form of consumption. It’s exactly as Allred says, it is a happy thing to do. I’m all for it, even if one motivation of it is that it will inevitably leak.</p>
<p>The part that matters is the second half, and given what I’ve seen on podcasts I have little doubt that Witt is accurate here:</p>
<blockquote><p>Stephen Witt: In truth, Mr. Huang was quite arrogant. He had little tolerance for disagreement and almost no capacity for open discourse. When you contradicted him, even over the most trivial matter, it was like lighting a fuse.</p>
<p>Stick to your position and soon he exploded, screaming at you for your idiocy. A harangue from Mr. Huang could last an hour or more; his executive staff appeared genuinely afraid of him.</p>
<p>Listening to their stories, I was reminded of the abusive music instructor from the movie “Whiplash.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Austen/status/2121279127307387119">Austen Allred</a>: “We went to Denny’s and I later learned he tipped the waitress $1,000. He didn’t let anyone know that, I only learned after I tracked the waitress down and asked how big the tip was.</p>



<p>I can only conclude from this that he was a very arrogant man masking his arrogance.”</p>



<p>Huh?&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zalberico/status/2121323546664382944">Zach Alberico</a>: I can only conclude from this the biographer is the worst kind of randian villian.</p></blockquote>
<p>This kind of ‘cult of the great man’ reaction is understandable in the face of the whiplash here, but neglects that Witt claims very strong direct evidence. He’s not saying that the tip itself was the evidence of arrogance.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/business/put-downs-parties-and-a-royal-family-the-culture-inside-jensen-huangs-nvidia-6a872194">We also have this article about Put-Downs, Parties and a ‘Royal Family</a>.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/byneilmehta/status/2122503821876486214">Neil Mehta</a>: this story is nuts</p>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/business/put-downs-parties-and-a-royal-family-the-culture-inside-jensen-huangs-nvidia-6a872194">Anissa Gardizy, Robbie Whelan, Katherine Long and Hannah Critchfield</a> (WSJ): The dressing downs Huang administered to employees, which one Nvidia executive described as “eviscerations,” had also become more frequent, according to people who have witnessed the episodes.</p>
<p>He had one such blowup during a recent “e-staff” meeting, an early-morning gathering of his executive staff, according to people who were present. After Vladimir Troy, a senior executive, discussed delays to Nvidia’s data-center builds, Huang excoriated Troy in front of the group, using abusive language.</p>
<p>“When Jensen does that, it is very painful. It’s crazy,” said one of the executives present. “He put Nvidia first, above any person.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2122686932127793584">Joe Weisenthal</a>: A lot of people say this is very common behavior among CEOs. But I’ve read enough articles to know that all of this sounds like prevalent behavior among anyone with direct reports, in both the public and private sectors.</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, yes, of course you put the company above any person. Or at least any person other than yourself. That’s how it works.</p>
<blockquote><p>Whole Mars Catalogue (quoting an article): People familiar with the matter say Jensen Huang’s “imperial control” extended to the Nvidia cafeteria, where he allegedly made employees try the brisket.</p>
<p>“He kept saying ‘you have to try this,’” one source said. “Nobody felt they could say no.”​</p></blockquote>
<p>And if the CEO tells you to try the brisket? You try the brisket.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2121293068716437774">PoliMath</a>: biographer on Jensen Huang: “I was reminded of the abusive music instructor from Whiplash”</p>



<p>ok, but he was the hero of that movie&nbsp;</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/dgv3yhxj78qdre5wy75y" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Whiplash is a great movie. The instructor is, in my view, neither hero nor villain. He simply is who he is, as is the student. He is a man who chooses the pursuit of greatness and is willing to have you pay the price for it. You can decide whether he is right to do that. The same applies to this aspect of Jensen Huang, or many other founders. They are often highly not fun people to be around. This can lead to greatness. You can decide what to think about that.</p>


<h4>Solve for the New Equilibrium</h4>


<p>I see why Dean Ball is taking the contrary position, but I fear that he is wrong.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2121137941980581996">Jeffrey Ladish</a> (Palisade Research): How hard would be it be for a future swarm of AI agents to take down someone like Jensen by leaking information about an affair or two?</p>
<p>People don’t understand how AI could take over without robots, often because they can’t model a well resourced, strategic adversary. Intelligence agency operations are a lower bound for what a strategic group of rogue agents could accomplish.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2121248146290586104">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): Quite hard, because by the time ai is good enough to autonomously perform that kind of intelligence collection, the capability will quickly diffuse broadly, many secrets of many people will have been exposed, and society will have recalibrated.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JeffLadish/status/2121316545712558235">Jeffrey Ladish</a> (Palisade Research): Why do you think that it will defuse broadly? I’m guessing you’re assuming weak RSI only here? When anyone gets to strong RSI, I’d expect these capabilities to rapidly move beyond anything human society has or could adapt to.</p>
<p>But even with ‘weak’ RSI, it seems quite plausible that one or several labs could have, or be had by, internal capabilities of this kind. Partially this is because I think the strong to weak spectrum is pretty spectrum-y, especially in worlds that don’t have a very fast foom, but I think you’d instead see an extremely steep slope of fairly smooth advances. But that would leave little time for either diffusion or adaptation, even if the capabilities are not yet strongly superhuman.</p></blockquote>
<p>The current situation is that many people, including very powerful people but also many ordinary people, have a bunch of secrets that could harm them quite a bit if weaponized. Often many of those secrets are not that hard to find. If you want to dig up dirt on someone, there is a good chance you can find it. If you are an intelligence agency, you can also create it by engineering the situation, including but not limited to honeypots, entrapment and scams, or of course you can also fabricate the information.</p>
<p>There are also any number of pieces of true information (e.g. your banking password) that you would very much not want others to know, even if you are doing nothing wrong. Or perhaps rather than you ‘doing something wrong’ what is being leaked are things like business secrets and logistical information, or your home address.</p>
<p>I think of this as analogous to cyber. Offense generally defeats defense under realistic conditions, because offense can concentrate firepower. You will not sufficiently harden defenses everywhere (which here would mean having no meaningful secrets) unless and until you expect to get hit for real. So Dean Ball’s adjustment probably only happens if you have anarchist-style or other groups out there trying to expose all the info like this on the regular, for the lulz or in principle or what not.</p>


<h4>But This Time They’ve Gone Too Far</h4>


<p>I do want to stand up for Jensen Huang in at least this small way: His plaque.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2121387056769405095">Andrew Curran</a>: The Jensen Huang commemorative plaque above the booth at the San Jose Denny’s where Jensen and his co-founders decided to start Nvidia has been removed.</p>
<p>Erased… from existence.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/v1xsoevuhyw4jaopc4vb" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/gs3saamgil8gykowhj5e" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>That action, in particular, is an outrage, assuming the story is true. This man earned that plaque.</p>
<p>Let us not take away from Jensen Huang <a href="https://www.thefp.com/p/tyler-cowen-how-much-is-a-ceo-worth-jensen-huang-nvidia">his thirty-three year history of building Nvidia</a>. There would be no Nvidia, certainly not in anything like its present form, without the efforts of Jensen Huang. He has created trillions in value, most of which did not come from his dealings with China and those smuggling chips to China or allowing them remote access, or his hard lobbying, regulatory capture and technical evasions of export controls. This was mostly a company that builds great products. Respect.</p>
<p>He is also responsible for his own downfall, and for choosing to sell so many chips to China when there was other demand for them, and for intentionally leaving Nvidia without an easy succession path. When we see the company decline in value by more than $570 billion in a week, that too is on him, not merely the lost opportunity from his leadership.</p>
<p>Let us remember him for all of it.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/T3oJfyHEy6uLEJ9FB/jensen-huang-really-liked-selling-china-advanced-ai-chips#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Almost-orthogonal directions can look circular in PCA plots](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/W9hyNv8EukB4WLniX/almost-orthogonal-directions-can-look-circular-in-pca-plots)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vectors that are arbitrarily close to orthogonal, but with a small circular correlation, will look circular when projected down to the first two principal component directions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This might be relevant to you if you are doing mech-interp and think you are seeing circular features.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Clade made this widget for me. Feel free to play around.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgment: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/philh?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@philh</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for proofreading this post. Thanks to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/lblack?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Lucius Bushnaq</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  for contributing one (out of four) sentences (not counting the acknowledgment section).</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/W9hyNv8EukB4WLniX/almost-orthogonal-directions-can-look-circular-in-pca-plots#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Read More Facts and Fewer Theories](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xyFdGGJ6Ckdnrqnsp/read-more-facts-and-fewer-theories)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m a broken record on this, but almost all of the most ~thoughtful~ people I know (or have interviewed) have memorized a lot of dates, places, names, numbers etc. And not just memorized, but care about their ability to recall specifics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Seek truth from facts. It’s the only way. — </span><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/1920216516475212040" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joe Wiesenthal</span></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So many people reason through heuristics, like who is aligned with whom, what personal financial interests they might have at stake, what peer group pressure they get. And I get it, because these things are complicated.</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But the way out, as always, is to seek truth from facts. — </span><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2099590984258457622" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joe Wiesenthal</span></a></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“You open the newspaper to an article on some subject you know well. In [Murray Gell-Mann’s] case, physics. In mine, show business. You read the article and see the journalist has absolutely no understanding of either the facts or the issues. … [Then you] turn the page to national or international affairs, and read with renewed interest as if the rest of the newspaper was somehow more accurate about far-off Palestine than it was about the story you just read. You turn the page, and forget what you know.” — </span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20100209064218/http://www.crichton-official.com/speech-whyspeculate.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Michael Crichton</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many who read about Diamond’s geographic determinism, Fukuyama’s end of history, Morris’s energy-capture thesis, Acemoglu and Robinson’s inclusive and extractive institutions, or Turchin’s structural-demographic theory would have been better served learning basic empirical facts about historical or present societies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are a bunch of reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With basic knowledge, you can reason from “first principles” rather than merely by weighing arguments or perspectives based on the brains and motives of their speakers.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Within an intellectual community, opinions are less correlated when people reason more on their own rather than defer. Otherwise it’s possible for the appearance of a universal opinion to hide its origins in a small number of independent thinkers.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you learn some facts, then you can quickly evaluate people’s general theories against the examples that you know well. One way to limit vulnerability to the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Crichton#%22Gell-Mann_amnesia_effect%22" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gell-Mann Amnesia Effect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is just to have immediate sanity checks on hand for more areas of knowledge.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Holden Karnofsky describes getting that last benefit as a result of his </span><a href="https://www.cold-takes.com/minimal-trust-investigations/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">minimal-trust investigations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—where he can determine which experts to trust more on a given large question based on whether they share his conclusions or generally exhibit good reasoning on the subquestions that he has evaluated in more depth—but basic empirical facts are much easier to accumulate than the complicated evaluations he talks about in that post, and so might make for a better sanity check that can discredit the propounders of many theories. Another benefit shared by the two practices is the ability to, as he says, get intuitions about “what sort of thing usually checks out” in social scientific theories or theories about human behavior or politics in general.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an example, if you are interested in AI timelines you could, instead of reading others’ arguments, ask Claude to draw charts of how much compute was used to train each model, or what revenue trends are over time, or how expensive it will be to build more compute, or other basic facts. If you are interested in a political topic like abortion, you might ask an LLM how many abortions are performed and at what gestational ages, what the distribution is of lifetime abortions across women, the age distributions thereof, etc. If you are interested in why the Democrats won the midterms in a landslide, you might ask for the trends in turnout and different voting patterns among different demographics, or polls about what voters in different demographics viewed as their top issues, and what candidates overperformed versus underperformed the overall shift, etc.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An interesting Tanner Greer post, “</span><a href="https://scholars-stage.org/a-study-guide-for-human-society-part-i/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Study Guide for Human Society</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,” advocates for studying in some depth three historical periods that vary in time and space, including one modern, one ancient, and one non-Western culture. So you might study Republican Rome, the Protestant Reformation, and 20th-century India. My intuition is that industrialized societies and more recent societies in general are more relevant than older ones, but the value of diversity might dominate.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your goal here is to build up a fairly granular knowledge of a particular time or event than can be called on to test and assess theories and narratives that will be thrown at you. “Famous scholar X proposes that y leads to z, but did y lead to z in each of the eras I am most familiar with?” You will know you have the background knowledge to do this right when you can answer questions like the following for a given era of expertise: “What are some of the biggest disagreements historians have about this era/event? What are the main sources historians or archaeologists use to try and understand the era, and how might they bias this understanding? If you had to pick one small incident or detail about the era that seems insignificant at first, but is actually very revealing example of the way this society/event worked, what would it be?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don’t need PhD levels answers to these questions. Just something more insightful that you would get from the Wikipedia page.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He gives this reading regimen as a prerequisite to more general histories or grand theories. </span><a href="https://x.com/benlandautaylor/status/1949560955190624261?t=vg0wHMI0-wtd9nsTxV2TKQ&amp;s=19" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ben Landau-Taylor says</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “If you want to understand how society works, you should spend more time reading about specific events and case studies than you spend reading about theories and explanations. Somewhere around 4:1 is roughly the right ratio.” I don’t know if that ratio is necessarily correct, but empirical facts seem overwhelmingly more important than general theories.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More generally, I think that the idea of rationality in general is somewhat overrated in places like LessWrong compared to the advantage of knowing a larger number of empirical facts (not only about history but also about natural or social sciences, philosophy, and so on). Without those foundations, it’s difficult to reach more accurate conclusions with lower cognitive bias alone.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xyFdGGJ6Ckdnrqnsp/read-more-facts-and-fewer-theories#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [It is mildly frustrating that people will now respect my faction less if ASI doesn't happen by 2028](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SaqXZbsLKjrSw6cwM/it-is-mildly-frustrating-that-people-will-now-respect-my)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By any reasonable definition I am an AI doomer. It has nevertheless been historically very strange for me to see how closely associated "AI doomers" and short timelines have been in the popular imagination. As anybody who's interacted with the AI risk case will realize, timelines are (at least in theory) mostly orthogonal with your beliefs on AI safety. Yes, timelines have some impact on what kinds of interventions are going to be viable, but among people who "believe in AGI" the variance in their optimism about the problem is more immediately dependent on underlying technical questions than anything else. Before GPT-2 or AlphaGo, there was still a much smaller, nascent "AI safety community", and these people were certainly not basing their worries on some data about AI uplift that we only got in 2026. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, for years people on general social media have taken it on faith that the "AI safety position" and "short timelines position" were the same, and even argued against short timelines </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as if </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in doing so they were arguing against "doomers." I actually don't think the AI safety people did much to promote this association in the beginning. Instead people would just assume that, if you were worried about AI, you must be worried about it happening in the next ten years, and therefore you must think LLMs scale to superintelligence all that there. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Personally, I don't have any inside view on how long it will take to get to superintelligence. I see remarkable progress, but I don't have a model for how optimizing verifiable metrics like pretraining loss perplexity or whatever will actually cash out into getting AIs to generalize much further, so I can't say whether current systems are going to get us to superintelligence, even if they can solve hard math problems. When I have encountered anti-short-timelines-conflated-with-AI-safety-people, I have historically responded</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbauss8gnz5f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbauss8gnz5f">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to them by explaining that we will </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eventually</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> develop superintelligence, even if it takes twenty years, and that most of the theoretical reasons you should be worried about AI alignment will probably still apply. I then make the separate and distinct observation </span><a href="https://www.betonit.ai/p/ai-bet" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/X3p8mxE5dHYDZNxCm/a-concrete-bet-offer-to-those-with-short-agi-timelines" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">skeptics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2122461910386061749" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://garymarcus.substack.com/p/why-the-collapse-of-the-generative" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">all</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://spectator.com/article/ai-just-exploded-again/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stripes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have essentially gotten the pace of AI progress wildly wrong, and so if your skepticism of AI risk is just that powerful AI will not happen soon, that seems really tenuous. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the last few years, though, it sure has gotten a lot harder to make that argument, because a group of people who represent the AI safety community:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bought a domain named "</span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2027</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Heavily marketed it as the work of the smartest, most rational, most well-informed people that we had to offer.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Told people in the first two paragraphs of the post that the timeline "represents [their] best guess about what superhuman AI looks like."</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tucked away inside a footnote that oh, by the way, this is just a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">modal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> timeline, and the median of most of the researchers involved is actually much longer than depicted, and actually the timeline got longer while they were writing it so it's slightly out of date.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was quite annoying to have to explain this last bullet to upwards of a dozen friends and relatives throughout the last two years, whom I do not think did anything wrong in getting a different impression. It was even more annoying to see the METR report come out, and almost everybody start to move toward ludicrously fast 6-12 month timelines based off of evidence that they're not even able to read. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suppose it's possible that these people are right, and if they're right then I salute them for their bravery and intelligence. But I can't help but feel personally annoyed that the attention economy is structured such that all of the people that state aggressive timelines end up representing the clique. The only AI predictions people remember, on both sides, are the extreme Ege Erdil/Daniel Kokotajlo-type predictions that are surprising and/or turn out extremely wrong. Mostly people just remember the year that was quoted and not the probability you attached to it. And the people we most disagree with, in general, tend to have ludicrously long timelines, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/K2D45BNxnZjdpSX2j/ai-timelines" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">extending out decades</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. So when people publish extremely short timeline estimates, especially when those estimates are unendorsed "modal" years, it feels like we are playing the "guess the number out of ten" game, our opponents picked "17", and we countered with "1". </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbauss8gnz5f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbauss8gnz5f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In real life, not on Twitter.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SaqXZbsLKjrSw6cwM/it-is-mildly-frustrating-that-people-will-now-respect-my#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Thoughts on "safety bad bc warning shots good"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qJrhEoNbzKRQYhybh/thoughts-on-safety-bad-bc-warning-shots-good)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose there was a button which made the current generation of AIs more safe, i.e. more aligned and less capable of subverting control measures. The button has no other effects — in particular, the button wouldn’t make future generations more or less safe. Should you press the button?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe you shouldn’t, because of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Warning Shot Argument</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Future agents contribute almost all the risk of catastrophe, compared with current agents.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pressing the button makes current agents safer.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If current agents are safer, then they are less likely to cause non-catastrophic harms, e.g. cybercrime.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If current agents don’t cause non-catastrophic harms, then people will be less aware about catastrophic risks, e.g. lab leadership, governments, legislators, international community, lab employees, general public, media, academia, etc.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If people are less aware about catastrophic risks, then they won’t take drastic action to reduce catastrophic risk.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So future agents contribute more catastrophic risk.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Therefore pressing the button would increase catastrophic risk.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This might seem very abstract, but consider a concrete example. Suppose you’d pressed the button to make OpenAI’s HPIM more aligned and less capable to subvert control measures. Then the HuggingFace incident would never have happened. And that probably would’ve increased catastrophic risk.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is this self-defeating?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The story is a little suspicious. In particular, it seems to account the second-order effect, i.e. “more safety -&gt; fewer warning shots -&gt; less awareness -&gt; less safety”. But ignores the higher-order effects like “more safety -&gt; fewer warning shots -&gt; less awareness -&gt; less safety -&gt; more warning shots -&gt; more awareness -&gt; more safety”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, maybe the second-order effect is bigger than the first-order effects and all higher-order effects. But it seems like that would involve pretty messy quantitive modelling that no one has grappled with.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, it seems very plausible that the HuggingFace incident actually increased catastrophic risk, because it caused labs to improve their control measures, therefore avoiding the bigger warning shots that would’ve occurred three months later.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My stance towards this is something like cluelessness? So I don’t put much stock in it.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qJrhEoNbzKRQYhybh/fmb34iwzvqdeo9ysifb1" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I made this diagram before the METR/Anthropic timelines report, so it places takeover in 2029.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other considerations</span></h1><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open-weight models will be a source of warning shots.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Now, the open-weight models are like 6 months behind the frontier. So the questions is what contributes more warning shots — open-weight models being misused, or closed-weight models being misaligned. Seems maybe like there’s a three month gap here — i.e. closed-source models contribute as much warning shot as open-source models three months later.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current AIs seem useful for reducing catastrophic risk.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In March 2025, Ryan Greenblatt wrote </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/ais-at-the-current-capability-level" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs at the current capability level may be important for future safety work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And that was more than 18 months ago. Current models are way more important for future safety work. In particular, they are useful for monitoring, grading, engineering, research, etc. In the HF investigation, he used huge amounts of AI labour for analysing the transcripts.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Demonstrating capabilities might be sufficient for awareness.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe we could raise awareness by demonstrating that the models </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">could</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> cause a catastrophe. For example, via penetration testing, scary demos, and capability evaluations.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Warning shots are scarier if they happen despite our best efforts to mitigate them.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> HF was scary because they escaped our sandboxes. OpenAI could’ve simply not used sandboxes whatsoever. But that would’ve made the incident less scary.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, my best guess is that you should press the button. But I wouldn’t be shocked if an oracle told me I shouldn’t.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qJrhEoNbzKRQYhybh/thoughts-on-safety-bad-bc-warning-shots-good#comments">Discuss</a>
